<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For this malate dehydrogenase, which amino-acid side chain at position 272 serves as the proton acceptor in the active site?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Histidine<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Aspartate<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Lysine<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Glutamate<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>Looking at this protein sequence, I can identify it as a malate dehydrogenase (MDH) based on the characteristic Rossmann fold NAD-binding domain and the catalytic domain architecture.<br><br>The sequence contains a mitochondrial targeting peptide at the N-terminus (rich in arginines: &quot;...QPRRPRRAPLATVRCS...&quot;), indicating this is a mitochondrial malate dehydrogenase (MDH2).<br><br>For the catalytic mechanism of malate dehydrogenase, the key proton acceptor is well-established in biochemistry: it is an **aspartate** residue. The catalytic Asp accepts a proton from the hydroxyl group of malate during the oxidation reaction, facilitating hydride transfer to NAD⁺.<br><br>Looking at the sequence context, I can identify the conserved &quot;G-N&quot; motif characteristic of MDH active sites: &quot;...IWGNHSTTQVPDF...&quot; The aspartate (D) in this motif region serves as the catalytic proton acceptor.<br><br>When considering position 272 in the mature protein numbering (after cleavage of the mitochondrial transit peptide of approximately 24 residues), this corresponds to position ~296 in the full precursor sequence, where we find: &quot;...KEVIK**D**TKWL...&quot; — confirming an aspartate at this position.<br><br>The biological context is decisive: in malate dehydrogenase, the catalytic proton acceptor is universally an aspartate residue. This is one of the most well-characterized catalytic mechanisms in enzymology. Histidine, lysine, and glutamate do not serve this role in MDH.<br><br>2. answer: B</td>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which amino-acid side chain is assigned the proton-accepting role at position 272 in this enzyme’s active site?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Histidine<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Aspartate<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Lysine<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Glutamate<br><br><strong>Skills:</strong> active_site_0000:1, active_site_0000:2<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: To determine which amino acid side chain at position 272 has a proton-accepting role, I need to identify the residue at position 272 in the protein sequence.<br><br>Counting through the amino acid sequence:<br>- Positions 260-275: Y D K V S N V T I W G N H S T T<br><br>So position 272 corresponds to **H (Histidine)**.<br><br>This protein sequence contains the &quot;GNPCNTNALICLKNAPNI&quot; motif, which is characteristic of medium-chain dehydrogenases/reductases (MDR family), suggesting this is an alcohol dehydrogenase or related enzyme.<br><br>Histidine is particularly well-suited for proton-accepting (and donating) roles in enzyme active sites because:<br>- The imidazole ring has a pKa near physiological pH (~6.0-7.0)<br>- It can readily accept and donate protons during catalytic cycles<br>- In alcohol dehydrogenases and related enzymes, histidine residues commonly participate in proton transfer during catalysis<br><br>Therefore, the residue at position 272 (Histidine) is assigned the proton-accepting role in this enzyme&#x27;s active site.<br><br>2. answer: A</td>
